| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Yemeni_crisis)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Yemeni_crisis)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Yemeni_crisis) |

On 26 March 2015, [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), leading a coalition of nine countries from West Asia and North Africa, staged a military intervention in [Yemen](350939.md) at the request of Yemeni president [Abdrabbuh Mansur Hadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdrabbuh_Mansur_Hadi), who had been ousted from the capital, [Sanaa](18691665.md), in September 2014 by [Houthi insurgents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_insurgency) during the [Yemeni civil war](46215853.md). Efforts by the [United Nations](31769.md) (UN) to facilitate a power sharing arrangement under a new transitional government collapsed, leading to escalating conflict between government forces, Houthi rebels, and other armed groups, which culminated in Hadi fleeing to Saudi Arabia shortly before it began military operations in the country.

The first month of the intervention, codenamed **Operation Decisive Storm** ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): عملية عاصفة الحزم, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *Amaliyyat 'Āṣifat al-Ḥazm*), consisted of airstrikes on Houthi rebels and a [full blockade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_Yemen).[^cite_note-86] On 22 April, the Saudi-led coalition declared that it had achieved its initial goals and announced **Operation Restoring Hope**, which would comprise a "combination of political, diplomatic and military action" while continuing "to prevent the Houthi militias from moving or undertaking any operations inside Yemen".[^cite_note-87] Ground forces were subsequently deployed into the country[^cite_note-88] as part of a broader offensive against both [Houthi](17240332.md) militants and loyalists of Hadi's predecessor, [Ali Abdullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Abdullah_Saleh).[^cite_note-89] Owing to Iran's support of these factions, the conflict is widely regarded as part of the broader [Saudi-Iran proxy conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_proxy_conflict).

[Egypt](8087628.md), [Morocco](19291.md), [Jordan](7515964.md), [Sudan](27421.md), and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) provided air and ground forces, while [Kuwait](7515890.md), [Qatar](8238258.md), [Bahrain](18933277.md), and U.S. private military contractor [Constellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constellis) headed several ground operations. [Djibouti](17207794.md), [Eritrea](17238590.md), and [Somalia](27358.md) opened their airspace, territorial waters, and military bases to coalition forces.[^cite_note-Ssfpstsciy-90] At varying stages, the intervention was backed by the United States, United Kingdom, France, Germany, and Canada. The U.S. provided intelligence and logistical support, such as [aerial refueling](237949.md) and search-and-rescue for downed coalition pilots,[^cite_note-hp-20160810-91] accelerated the sale of weapons to coalition states,[^cite_note-92] and continued [strikes against AQAP.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_drone_strikes_in_Yemen) American and British servicemen have provided advice and training related to Saudi-led airstrikes in Yemen.[^cite_note-theguardian-com_2016-01-15_BZM-93][^cite_note-theguardian-com_2016-01-19_BHS-94][^cite_note-publications-parliament-uk_2016-01-12_HoC-95]

The intervention drew criticism from some governments, international organizations, and human rights groups, which stated that it contributed to civilian casualties, damage to infrastructure, and the humanitarian situation in Yemen.[^cite_note-un-org_2015-07-28_DCN-96][^cite_note-auto9-97] Academics also dispute whether it violates [Article 2(4)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Use_of_force_in_international_law) of the [UN Charter](88805.md).[^cite_note-98][^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100] By 2019, the conflict was reported as a "military stalemate",[^cite_note-101] and the following year, Saudi Arabia declared its first unilateral ceasefire.[^cite_note-covid-102][^cite_note-103] On 29 March 2022, the Saudi-led coalition announced that it would cease all hostilities within Yemen to facilitate political talks and peacekeeping efforts;[^cite_note-104] Houthi and Saudi officials subsequently began bilateral peace talks mediated by [Oman](22316.md) under UN auspices, and most restrictions on commercial goods were lifted by April 2023.[^cite_note-\:0-105] As of April 2024, open hostilities have largely ceased, though negotiations are ongoing due to complications caused by [Houthi attacks on Red Sea shipping](75207766.md) since October 2023.[^cite_note-\:0-105] After Saudi Arabia [resumed bombing Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Sanaa_airport_strike) amidst the [2026 Iran war](82533582.md), [Ansar Allah](17240332.md) launched [an offensive](84167596.md) and succeeded in capturing territory along Yemen's western coast,[^cite_note-106] potentially giving the group control over the geostrategically important [Bab-el-Mandeb](62138.md) strait.

## Background

See also: [Saudi Arabia–Yemen relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabia%E2%80%93Yemen_relations), [Houthi insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_insurgency), and [Aftermath of the Houthi takeover in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_the_Houthi_takeover_in_Yemen)

Saudi-backed [Abdrabbuh Mansur Hadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdrabbuh_Mansur_Hadi), running unopposed as the only candidate for president, won the [2012 Yemeni elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Yemeni_presidential_election).[^cite_note-107] Since August 2014, the [Houthis](17240332.md) (also known as Ansar Allah) a [Zaidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaidiyyah) [Shia](26961.md) movement and militant group backed by [Iran](14653.md), organized mass protests against the Hadi government's decisions and the new constitution. The protests culminated into the [Houthi takeover of the Yemeni government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi_takeover_in_Yemen) in 2015, after which the Houthis declared victory for the revolution and began drafting a new constitution, saying that Hadi's provisional government's term had already expired. Saudi Arabia and other countries denounced this as an unconstitutional coup d'état.[^cite_note-108] The Houthis were supported by sections of the Yemeni armed forces loyal to the former president [Ali Abdullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Abdullah_Saleh), who was removed from power as part of the 2011 Arab Spring uprisings[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110] but was later killed by his former Houthi allies.[^cite_note-111]

By September 2014, Houthi fighters captured [Sanaa](18691665.md), toppling Hadi's government. Soon after, a peace deal (known as the 'Peace and Partnership Agreement') was sealed between the Hadi government and the Houthis, but was not honored by either party. The deal was drafted with the intent of defining a power-sharing government. A conflict over a draft constitution resulted in the Houthis consolidating control over the Yemeni capital in January 2015. After resigning from his post alongside his prime minister and remaining under virtual house arrest for one month, Hadi fled to [Aden](38050.md) in [southern Yemen](718749.md) in February.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] Upon arriving in Aden, Hadi withdrew his resignation, saying that the actions of the Houthis from September 2014 had amounted to a "coup" against him.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115] By 25 March, forces answering to Sanaa were [rapidly closing in on Aden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aden_\(2015\)), which Hadi had declared to be Yemen's temporary capital.[^cite_note-116]

During the [Houthis' southern offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemeni_Civil_War_\(2015%E2%80%93present\)#Southern_offensive), Saudi Arabia began a military buildup on its [border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabia%E2%80%93Yemen_border) with Yemen.[^cite_note-117] In response, a Houthi commander boasted that his troops would counterattack against any Saudi aggression and would not stop until they had taken [Riyadh](51554.md), the Saudi capital.[^cite_note-118]

On 25 March, Hadi called on the UN Security Council to authorise "willing countries that wish to help Yemen to provide immediate support for the legitimate authority by all means and measures to protect Yemen and deter the Houthi aggression".[^cite_note-119]

Yemen's foreign minister, [Riad Yassin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riad_Yassin), requested military assistance from the [Arab League](52625.md) on 25 March, amid reports that Hadi had fled his provisional capital.[^cite_note-120][^cite_note-121] On 26 March, Saudi state TV station [Al-Ekhbariya TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ekhbariya_TV) reported that Hadi arrived at a Riyadh airbase and was met by Saudi defense minister [Mohammed bin Salman](38689358.md). His route from Aden to Riyadh was not immediately known.[^cite_note-CBC_News-122]

At a summit of the Arab League held in Sharm El-Sheikh, Egypt, on 28–29 March, President Hadi again repeated his calls for international intervention in the fighting. A number of League members pledged their support to Hadi's government during that meeting.[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124]

After more than seven years of hostilities that left thousands of civilians dead or injured and devastated [Yemen](350939.md), a surprise deal was agreed between regional rivals Saudi Arabia and Iran in March 2023. The deal aimed to restore diplomatic relations that could end the Saudi war in Yemen. Experts raised a warning that the war could worsen instead of ending, if Saudi Arabia agrees to end military operations.[^cite_note-125]

## Operation Decisive Storm

Not to be confused with [Operation Desert Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Desert_Storm).

According to the Saudi news outlet [Al Arabiya](321645.md), Saudi Arabia contributed 100 warplanes and 150,000 soldiers to the military operation. Several media agencies reported that planes from [Egypt](8087628.md), [Morocco](19291.md), [Jordan](7515964.md), [Sudan](27421.md), [Kuwait](7515890.md), the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md), [Qatar](8238258.md) and [Bahrain](18933277.md) were taking part.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127][^cite_note-strength-128][^cite_note-repel2-129][^cite_note-sudan-130][^cite_note-131] Egypt had previously sent four warships supporting the Saudi naval blockade.[^cite_note-sites.tufts.edu-132]

The operation was declared over on 21 April 2015.[^cite_note-133]

### Air campaign

#### March 2015

In March 2015, in a joint statement, the member states of the [Gulf Cooperation Council](220950.md) (with the exception of [Oman](22316.md)) said they had decided to intervene against the Houthis at the request of Hadi's government.[^cite_note-134]

The coalition declared Yemeni airspace to be a restricted area, with [King Salman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salman_of_Saudi_Arabia) declaring the [Royal Saudi Air Force](570367.md) to be [in full control of the zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No-fly_zone).[^cite_note-repel2-129] Saudi Arabia began airstrikes, reportedly relying on US intelligence reports and surveillance images to select and hit targets, including weapons, aircraft[^cite_note-cnn-135] on the ground and air defences.[^cite_note-136] [Al Jazeera](30204052.md) reported that [Mohammed Ali al-Houthi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Ali_al-Houthi), a Houthi commander appointed in February as president of the [Revolutionary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Committee_\(Yemen\)), was injured and three other Houthi commanders were killed by airstrikes in Sanaa.[^cite_note-137]

Strikes on 26 March also hit [Al Anad Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Anad_Air_Base), a former [US special operations forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_special_operations_forces) facility in [Lahij Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahij_Governorate) seized by Houthis earlier in the week.[^cite_note-leads-138] The targets reportedly included the Houthi-controlled missile base in Sanaa and its fuel depot.[^cite_note-offensive2-139] Strikes overnight also targeted Houthis in [Taiz](545270.md) and [Saada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saada). Thousands demonstrated in Sanaa against the intervention, which ex-president [Ali Abdullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Abdullah_Saleh) also condemned. In Taiz thousands came out supporting Hadi and Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-140]

The scope of strikes expanded further on 27 March, with a radar installation in the [Marib Governorate](3604391.md) and an airbase in the [Abyan Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abyan_Governorate) coming under air attack. The commander of the operation dismissed reports of civilian casualties, saying airstrikes were being carried out with precision.[^cite_note-voa-141] Additional strikes early on the next day hit targets in [Hodeidah](769308.md), Saada and the Sanaa area, as well as [Ali Abdullah Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Abdullah_Saleh)'s main base. Rumours indicated Saleh fled to [Sanhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanhan,_Yemen), on the outskirts of the Houthi-controlled capital.[^cite_note-142] An Aden government official said Saudi strikes destroyed a long-range missile facility controlled by the Houthis.[^cite_note-143]

The Houthis claimed to have shot down a [Sudanese Air Force](550818.md) plane over northern Sanaa and captured its pilot on 28 March. The Sudanese government denied that any of its four warplanes had come under fire or been shot down.[^cite_note-sudan-130] On the previous day, the Houthis claimed to have shot down a "hostile" Saudi drone in Sanaa.[^cite_note-144]

Airstrikes hit an arms depot, military airbase and special forces headquarters in Sanaa early on 29 March. A weapons depot outside Sanaa was destroyed, causing damage to an airport and planes on the ground. Saada and Hodeidah were targeted as well. Brigadier General [Ahmed Asiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Asiri_\(military\)), the coalition's spokesman, said Saudi artillery and [Apache](37746.md) attack helicopters were mobilised to "deter" Houthi fighters massing on the border with Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-145]

On 30 March, at least 40 people including children were killed and 200 were injured[^cite_note-146] by an airstrike that hit the Al-Mazraq refugee camp near a military installation in northern district of [Haradh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haradh), international organizations said. Airstrikes also hit areas near the presidential palace in Sanaa,[^cite_note-147] as well as [Aden International Airport](9421129.md).[^cite_note-dozens-148]

Food storage of Yemen Economic Corporation in Hodeidah was destroyed by three coalition strikes on 31 March.[^cite_note-149] Airstrikes were not limited to the Yemeni mainland. Missiles struck homes on the island of [Perim](1907645.md), according to residents who fled by boat to [Djibouti](17207794.md).[^cite_note-150]

#### April 2015

Destruction in Sanaa after airstrike on 20 April 2015



Destruction in the residential neighborhoods near Attan mountain



Destroyed shopping center

Dozens of casualties came from an explosion in a dairy and oil factory in [Hodeidah](769308.md), which was variously blamed on an airstrike or a rocket from a nearby military base on 1 April. Medical sources reported 25 deaths, while the Yemen Army said 37 were killed and 80 wounded.[^cite_note-151] Airstrikes also hit targets in Saada on 1 April.[^cite_note-rages-152]

Despite persistent airstrikes, Houthi and allied units continued to advance on central Aden, backed by tanks and heavy artillery.[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] Houthis seized the presidential palace on 2 April, but reportedly withdrew after overnight air raids early the next day. Coalition planes also airdropped weapons and medical aid to pro-Hadi fighters in Aden.[^cite_note-155]

The [International Committee of the Red Cross](5023832.md) (ICRC) announced on 5 April that it had received permission from the coalition to fly medical supplies and aid workers into Sanaa and was awaiting permission to send a surgical team by boat to [Aden](38050.md). The coalition said it had set up a special body to coordinate aid deliveries to Yemen.[^cite_note-156]

On 6 April, airstrikes began before sunset and struck targets in western Sanaa, Saada and the [Ad Dali' Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ad_Dali'_Governorate), a supply route for Houthis in the [Battle of Aden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aden_\(2015\)).[^cite_note-fiercefighting-157]

Airstrikes on 7 April hit a [Republican Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Guard_\(Yemen\)) base in the [Ibb Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibb_Governorate), injuring 25 troops. Yemeni sources claimed three children at a nearby school were killed by the attack,[^cite_note-158] while six were injured.[^cite_note-159]

The [Parliament of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Pakistan) voted against military action on 10 April, despite a request from Saudi Arabia that it join the coalition.[^cite_note-160]

Airstrikes launched on 12 April, against the base of the 22nd Brigade of the [Yemeni Republican Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Guard_\(Yemen\)) in the [Taiz Governorate](3604497.md) struck both the brigade and a nearby village inhabited by members of the [Al-Akhdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Akhdam) minority community, killing eight civilians and injuring more than ten others.[^cite_note-161] On 17 April, both the GCC coalition's spokesman called by Saudi broadcaster Al-Ehkbariya TV and a commander of the pro-Hadi rebels on the ground said airstrikes had intensified, focusing on both Sanaa and Taiz.[^cite_note-162] One strike on the Republican Palace in [Taiz](545270.md) killed 19 pro-Houthi gunmen.[^cite_note-163]



Ethnoreligious groups in 2002. [Zaydi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaidiyyah) [Shia](26961.md) followers make up between 35% and 42.1% of Muslims in Yemen.

[^cite_note-164]

### Naval role

[Egypt](8087628.md) and Saudi Arabia committed warships to support coalition operations.[^cite_note-165] Somalia offered its airspace and territorial waters.[^cite_note-Ssfpstsciy-90] Four [Egyptian Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Navy) vessels steamed toward the [Gulf of Aden](58716.md) after operations began.[^cite_note-leads-138] Riyadh requested access to Somali airspace and waters to carry out operations.[^cite_note-166] On 27 March, the [Egyptian military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_of_Egypt) said a squadron of Egyptian and Saudi warships took up positions at the [Bab al-Mandab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bab_al-Mandab) strait.[^cite_note-voa-141] The Saudi military threatened to destroy any ship attempting to make port.[^cite_note-167]

The [Royal Saudi Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Saudi_Navy) evacuated diplomats and United Nations staff from Aden to [Jeddah](277718.md) on 28 March.[^cite_note-flee-168]

Witnesses told [Reuters](18998750.md) that Egyptian warships bombarded Houthi positions as they attempted to advance on Aden on 30 March.[^cite_note-169] Warships again fired on Houthi positions at Aden International Airport on or about 1 April.[^cite_note-rages-152]

Djibouti foreign minister [Mahmoud Ali Youssouf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_Ali_Youssouf) said the Houthis placed heavy weapons and fast attack boats on [Perim](1907645.md) and a smaller island in the Bab al-Mandab strait. He warned "the prospect of a war in the strait of Bab al-Mandab is a real one" and said the weapons posed "a big danger" to his country, commercial shipping traffic, and military vessels. He called on the coalition to clear the islands, which he said included missiles and long-range cannons.[^cite_note-youssouf-170]

On 4 April, Egyptian President [Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md) called protecting [Red Sea](26191.md) shipping and securing the Bab al-Mandab "a top priority for Egypt's national security".[^cite_note-171]

On 15 April, coalition spokesman Saudi Brigadier General Ahmed Al-Asiri, said that its warships were focusing on protecting shipping routes and screening ships heading to port for shipments intended for the Houthis.[^cite_note-172]

The [US Navy](20518076.md) provided support to the naval blockade, halting and searching vessels suspected of carrying Iranian arms to the Houthis.[^cite_note-173] On 21 April, the United States announced it was deploying warships to Yemeni waters to monitor Iranian ships.[^cite_note-174] The US in particular noted a convoy of Iranian vessels, which US authorities said could potentially be carrying weapons to Houthi fighters in contravention of UN sanctions.[^cite_note-175] The US reported that the Iranian convoy reversed course on 23 April.[^cite_note-176]

### Ground clashes

Sudan said it was stationing ground troops in Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-177] The Special Forces of the [Bahrain Defence Force](67658.md), Taskforce 11, were also deployed to Yemen.[^cite_note-178]

Between 31 March and April, Saudi and Houthi forces reportedly traded artillery and rocket fire across the border between SA and Yemen.[^cite_note-dozens-148][^cite_note-179] A Saudi border guard was killed on 2 April, the campaign's first confirmed coalition casualty.[^cite_note-180] Followed by another two soldiers killed the next day.[^cite_note-provinces-181] An Egyptian truck driver was killed by Houthi shelling.[^cite_note-182]

SA reportedly began removing sections of the [Saudi–Yemen barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi%E2%80%93Yemen_barrier) fence along its border with the Saada and [Hajjah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hajjah_Governorate) [governorates](690277.md) on 3 April. The purpose of the removal was not immediately clear.[^cite_note-remove-183]

On 12 April, members of the Takhya tribe launched an attack on a Saudi base after several of its members died in an airstrike. Weapons and ammunition were taken.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186]

On 19 April, as Houthi leader Abdul-Malek El-Houthi accused SA of planning to invade Yemen,[^cite_note-Reuters-187] Asiri claimed that coalition forces had information regarding a planned Houthi incursion into SA.[^cite_note-188] A Saudi border guard died on 19 April and two others were injured from gunfire and mortar shelling across the border.[^cite_note-189]

## Operation Restoring Hope



King [Salman of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salman_of_Saudi_Arabia) and Saudi Foreign Minister Adel al-Jubeir meet with [U.S. Secretary of State John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) in September 2015.

On 21 April 2015, the [Saudi Defence Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_\(Saudi_Arabia\)) declared it was ending the campaign of airstrikes because it had "successfully eliminated the threat" to its security posed by Houthi ballistic and heavy weaponry.[^cite_note-190] It announced the start of a new phase codenamed Operation Restoring Hope.[^cite_note-191] In a televised address, Hadi said the end of airstrikes had come at his request and thanked the Arab coalition for their support.[^cite_note-bbc.com:_Iran_urges_aid_effort-192]

Earlier that day King Salman ordered the Saudi National Guard to join the military operation.[^cite_note-193] Air and naval strikes continued despite the announcement that *Decisive Storm* had ended.

Both the Omani[^cite_note-omanpeace-194] and Iranian[^cite_note-bbc.com:_Iran_urges_aid_effort-192][^cite_note-195] governments said they welcomed the end of airstrikes. On 22 April, Oman presented a seven-point peace deal to both parties. The proposed peace treaty entailed the reinstatement of Hadi's government and the evacuation of Houthi fighters from major cities.[^cite_note-omanpeace-194]

**Outside intervention**

On 8 May, Saudi Arabia announced a five-day ceasefire set to start on 12 May,[^cite_note-196] following heavy pressure from the US.[^cite_note-197] Later in the day, Saudi airplanes dropped leaflets in the [Saada Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saada_Governorate) warning of airstrikes throughout the area.[^cite_note-198] Houthi spokesman Mohamed al-Bukhaiti later told the [BBC](19344654.md) that the ceasefire had not been formally proposed and the Houthis would not respond until a plan was properly laid out.[^cite_note-199] A spokesman for the Houthi-aligned military announced agreement to the ceasefire plan on 10 May, although he warned that a breach of the truce would prompt a military response.[^cite_note-200]

On 13 May, humanitarian agencies said they were trying to get aid into Yemen after a five-day ceasefire took effect on Tuesday night. Ships carrying humanitarian supplies docked at the Houthi-controlled [Red Sea](26191.md) port of [Hodeidah](769308.md) as planes were standing by to help evacuate the injured.[^cite_note-201] Meanwhile, King Salman doubled his country's Yemen aid pledge to \$540 million, funds the UN said would "meet the life-saving and protection needs of 7.5 million people affected".[^cite_note-202]

### Airstrikes

At the operation's announcement, coalition leadership stressed that their campaign would attempt a political solution and that they would continue the air and naval blockade.[^cite_note-203] Airstrikes resumed almost immediately following the coalition's announcement of the end of Operation Decisive Storm.[^cite_note-204]

On 22 April airstrikes continued in [Taiz](545270.md), where an army base was hit shortly after Houthi fighters took it over,[^cite_note-npr-shifts-205] and Aden, where an airstrike targeted Houthi tanks moving into a contested district,[^cite_note-206] among other locations, such as Hodeidah and [Ibb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibb).[^cite_note-207] The Houthis continued to fight for territory,[^cite_note-npr-shifts-205] with a Houthi spokesman saying the group would be prepared for peace talks on the condition of "a complete halt of attacks". The previous round of UN-sponsored talks collapsed after Houthi rebels attacked Hadi's residence in Sanaa.[^cite_note-208]

By 26 April, coalition forces were striking what they described as Houthi military targets in Sanaa and Aden and in other locations, notably in Sa'ada province near the Saudi border, nearly every night.[^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210] On 26 April, after midnight, airstrikes struck Houthi and pro-Saleh positions and targets in and around Sanaa, Aden, and the [Marib](3604391.md) and [Ad Dali'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ad_Dali'_Governorate) governorates, backing up anti-Houthi fighters in the latter three locations, with more than 90 rebels reportedly killed.[^cite_note-211] Coalition warships shelled fighters near Aden's commercial port. Saudi warplanes also targeted Houthis in the Saada Governorate, while Saudi artillery fired on targets in the Hajjah Governorate along the border.[^cite_note-212] The Saudi National Guard was deployed on the border.[^cite_note-213]

On 28 April, [Sanaa International Airport](4202141.md) was bombed[^cite_note-214] by Saudi F-15 fighters to prevent an Iranian plane[^cite_note-215] belonging to [Iranian Red Crescent Society](20826914.md) (IRCS) from landing, while it was approaching to land. The fighters had warned the plane to turn back, in an unsuccessful attempt to thwart its landing, but the Iranian pilot ignored the "illegal warnings", saying that, on the basis of international law, his plane did not need further permission to land.[^cite_note-216]

On the night of 6 May 2015, the Saudi-led coalition carried out 130 airstrikes in Yemen in a 24-hour period. At first, coalition spokesperson Ahmed Asiri admitted that schools and hospitals were targeted but claimed that these were used as weapon storage sites. Asiri later claimed that his words had been mistranslated. The United Nations humanitarian coordinator for Yemen Johannes Van Der Klaauw said that these bombings constituted a [war crime](48626.md). "The [indiscriminate bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiscriminate_attack) of populated areas, with or without prior warning, is a contravention international humanitarian law," he said. He continued to say that he was particularly concerned about airstrikes on Saada "where scores of civilians were reportedly killed and thousands were forced to flee their homes after the coalition declared the entire governate a military target".[^cite_note-edition.cnn.com-217]



Saudi foreign minister [Adel al-Jubeir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adel_al-Jubeir) announced a five-day ceasefire in Yemen, 8 May 2015.

The [Iranian Foreign Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Foreign_Ministry) summoned the Saudi [chargé d'affaires](856252.md), and the [Iranian Parliament](383276.md) and the Iranian Red Crescent Society blasted Saudi Arabia for blocking Iranian humanitarian aid.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219]

The UN [Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_for_the_Coordination_of_Humanitarian_Affairs) (OCHA) "strongly urged" the coalition to stop targeting airports and seaports so that aid could reach all Yemenis.[^cite_note-reuters-airport-220][^cite_note-221]

ICRC and [Médecins Sans Frontières](20498.md) (MSF), also known as Doctors Without Borders, said that they were extremely concerned about damage to the airports at Sanaa and to the port city of [Hodeidah](769308.md).[^cite_note-reuters-airport-220]

Overnight on 29 and 30 April, Saudi Arabia was reported to have airdropped arms to anti-Houthi fighters in Taiz.[^cite_note-ReferenceB-222]

On 30 April, airstrikes hit five provinces.[^cite_note-ReferenceB-222] New airstrikes hit SIA, completely halting aid deliveries.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-223]



An airstrike in Sanaa, 11 May 2015

On 6 May, coalition airstrikes targeted the Police Training Center in the [Dhamar Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhamar_Governorate), damaging nearby houses[^cite_note-Saba_Net_–_Yemen_news_agency-224] meanwhile the civil aviation authority announced it would re-open the airport to receive aid.[^cite_note-225]

Coalition airstrikes targeted the houses of Saleh in Sanaa in the early hours of 10 May, eyewitnesses said. Khabar, a Yemeni news agency allied with Saleh said that the former president and his family were unharmed.[^cite_note-226]

The Moroccan government said on 10 May that one of its [General Dynamics F-16 Fighting Falcon](11642.md) aircraft taking part in the air campaign went missing in action over Yemen, along with its pilot.[^cite_note-227] The Houthis claimed responsibility, with Yemeni state TV broadcasting a report on the jet being downed by tribal militias over the Saada Governorate and showing images of the wreckage.[^cite_note-airloss-228]

On 18 May, Saudi-led airstrikes reportedly resumed on Houthi positions after a humanitarian ceasefire expired late the previous day; three coalition airstrikes hit [Saada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saada). Yemen's exiled Foreign Minister Riyadh Yassin blamed the rebel group for the renewal of hostilities. Al-Arabiya said Saudi forces shelled Houthi outposts along Yemen's northern border after the fighters fired mortars at a Saudi army post in Najran province.[^cite_note-229]

On 23 May, OCHA reported that airstrikes continued in the northern governorates of Sa'ada (Baqim, Haydan, Saqayn and As Safra) and Hajjah (Abs, Hayran, Haradh, Huth, Kuhlan Affar and Sahar districts). The road connecting Haradh and Huth districts was reportedly hit. Airstrikes were also reported in Al Jawf Governorate (Bart Al Anan district).[^cite_note-230]

On 27 May, airstrikes hit a police station in the capital, [Sanaa](18691665.md), killing 45 officers.[^cite_note-231] The Houthi-controlled Ministry of Health announced that in total, 96 people were killed.

On 3 June, the residence of a Houthi leader in Ibb province was hit by an airstrike, according to eyewitnesses.[^cite_note-232]

Destruction in the south of Sanaa (12 June 2015)



Destroyed house



Destroyed car

On 12 June, Saudi jets bombed the [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md) of Sanaa Old City, killing at least six people and destroying some of the ancient buildings. [UNESCO](21786641.md) Director General [Irina Bokova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irina_Bokova) said in a statement that she is "profoundly distressed by the loss of human lives as well as by damage inflicted on one of the world's oldest jewels of Islamic urban landscape". Locals also condemned the action.[^cite_note-233]

On 23 September 2015, the Saudi-led coalition destroyed a ceramics factory in the town of Matnah. One civilian was killed and others were wounded. According to the BBC, the bomb is believed to have been produced in the United Kingdom by GEC-Marconi Dynamics.[^cite_note-234] The factory's owner Ghalib al-Sawary told the BBC: "We built it over 20 years but to destroy it took only twenty minutes."[^cite_note-auto2-235] Campaigners say this attack was a violation of the laws of war.

On 26 October 2015 Médecins Sans Frontières reported that a coalition airstrike had completely destroyed a hospital they ran in Saada province's Haydan governorate, including the operating room. When the first strike hit an unused part of the hospital the facility was completely evacuated, so there were no direct casualties. A spokesman for the coalition forces, Brig-Gen [Ahmed al-Asiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Asiri), denied responsibility for the attack.[^cite_note-236] "With the hospital destroyed, at least 200,000 people now have no access to lifesaving medical care," MSF said. "This attack is another illustration of a complete disregard for civilians in Yemen, where bombings have become a daily routine," said Hassan Boucenine, MSF head of mission in Yemen. The GPS coordinates of the only hospital in the Haydan district were regularly shared with the Saudi-led coalition, and the roof of the facility was clearly identified with the MSF logo, he said.[^cite_note-237] UNICEF said the hospital in Saada was the 39th health center hit in Yemen since March, when the violence escalated. "More children in Yemen may well die from a lack of medicines and healthcare than from bullets and bombs," its executive director [Anthony Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Lake) said in a statement. He added that critical shortages of fuel, medication, electricity and water could mean many more will close. [Amnesty International](18947898.md) said the strike may amount to a war crime and called for an independent investigation.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239]

In February 2016, the Saudis bombed the ancient citadel of Kawkaban, killing seven civilians.[^cite_note-auto2-235]

On 8 October 2016, Saudi-led airstrikes [targeted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanaa_funeral_airstrike) a hall in Sanaa where a funeral was taking place. At least 140 people were killed and about 600 were wounded. According to *The Independent*, one rescuer said: "The place has been turned into a lake of blood."[^cite_note-independent.co.uk-240] After initially denying it was behind the attack, the Coalition's Joint Incidents Assessment Team admitted that it had bombed the hall but claimed that this attack had been a mistake caused by bad information.[^cite_note-241] After this attack, US national security spokesperson said that the US government was "deeply disturbed" by the bombing and added that US support for the Saudi-led coalition was "not a blank cheque". He added "we have initiated an immediate review of our already significantly reduced support to the Saudi-led Coalition."[^cite_note-independent.co.uk-240] The United Nations humanitarian co-ordinator in Yemen Jamie McGoldrick said he was "shocked and outraged" by the "horrific" bombing. "This violence against civilians in Yemen must stop," he said.[^cite_note-independent.co.uk-240]

On the night of 15 February 2017, the Saudi-led coalition bombed a funeral reception near Sanaa. Initial reports suggest the bombing killed nine women and one child with ten more women reported wounded. "People heard the sound of planes and started running from the house but then the bombs hit the house directly. The roof collapsed and there was blood everywhere," a resident of the village told a Reuters news agency cameraman.[^cite_note-242]

An explosion in a warehouse on Sunday 7 April 2019, in Sanaa, have killed at least 11 civilians, including school children and left more than 39 people wounded. The [Associated Press](18935732.md) news agency said 13 killed, including 7 children and more than 100 were wounded. According to Al Jazeera and Houthi officials, the civilians were killed in a Saudi-led coalition airstrike.[^cite_note-243] The Saudi-led coalition denied any airstrikes took place that day on Sanaa. The state-run news agency in Aden, aligned with the internationally recognized government, said the rebels had stored weapons at the warehouse. According to *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*, "some families and residents of the district of Sawan said the explosion occurred after a fire erupted inside the warehouse. They said a fire sent columns of white smoke rising into the air, followed by the explosion." Their accounts were confirmed by several videos filmed by bystanders.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245]

#### Aircraft losses

Main article: [List of aviation shootdowns and accidents during the Saudi Arabian–led intervention in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_aviation_shootdowns_and_accidents_during_the_Saudi_Arabian%E2%80%93led_intervention_in_Yemen)

### Cross-border fighting

Main article: [Houthi–Saudi Arabian conflict](47695166.md)

### Ground combat

See also: [Battle of Aden (2015)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Aden_\(2015\))

On 3 April 2015, [CNN](62028.md) cited an unnamed Saudi source who claimed that Saudi special forces were on the ground in and around [Aden](38050.md), "coordinating and guiding" the resistance.[^cite_note-246] The Saudi government officially declined to comment on whether it had [special forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_forces), with Saudi Ambassador to the United States [Adel al-Jubeir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adel_al-Jubeir) saying on 2 April that Saudi Arabia had no "formal" troops in Aden.[^cite_note-remove-183]

The Battle of Aden came to an end with pro-Hadi forces again seized control of Aden port and moving into the city's commercial center.[^cite_note-Mohammed_Mukhashaf-247][^cite_note-Glen_Carey-248] On 22 July, pro-Hadi forces had retaken full control of Aden, and the Aden Airport was reopened. In late July, an offensive launched by pro-Hadi forces drove Houthi forces out of the towns neighboring Aden.[^cite_note-offensive2-139]

On 4 September a Houthi [OTR-21 Tochka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OTR-21_Tochka) missile hit an ammunition dump at a military base in Safer in Ma'rib Governorate killing 52 UAE, 10 Saudi and 5 Bahraini soldiers. The Safer base was being built up by coalition forces for a push against Sanaa.[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251] "It was the deadliest single attack on coalition soldiers since the start of its operation against Houthi rebels in March" Asseri said.[^cite_note-252] The attacked was the highest casualty loss in the history of the UAE military.[^cite_note-253] Qatar deployed 1000 troops to Yemen after the incident.[^cite_note-254]

By 8 September it was reported that the Saudi-led forces deployed in Yemen exceeded 10,000 troops and included 30 [AH-64 Apache](37746.md) attack helicopters.[^cite_note-255]

On 14 December media reported a Houthi & Saleh Forces [missile attack at a Saudi military camp south-west of the besieged city of Taiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_2015_Taiz_missile_attack),[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257] while sources confirmed the killings of over 150 coalition soldiers including 23 Saudi troops, 9 UAE officers and soldiers, 7 Moroccan soldiers and 42 Blackwater troops.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259]

On 19 December 2015, reported clashes leaves over 40 Houthi rebels and 35 government loyalists dead, with dozens wounded on both sides.[^cite_note-260]

In June 2018, anti-Houthi forces led by Saudi Arabia and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) assaulted the port of [Hodeidah](769308.md),[^cite_note-261] in an effort to dislodge Houthi forces.[^cite_note-262]

In January 2022, Yemeni troops backed by the Saudi-led coalition took control of the entire [Shabwah Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabwah_Governorate) from the Houthis.[^cite_note-263] This operation was supported and directed by the United Arab Emirates.[^cite_note-264]

### Naval involvement

Main article: [Blockade of Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_Yemen)

**Estimated fuel needs in Yemen and monthly fuel imports**

Saudi Arabia faced growing criticism for the Saudi-led naval and air blockade, which effectively isolated the country.[^cite_note-NYT_2015-05-12_CFY-269]

A "military source and pro-Hadi militiamen" told the AFP on 26 April 2015 that coalition warships were participating in the shelling of Aden.[^cite_note-270]

On 30 April, the Iranian navy announced it had deployed two destroyers to the Gulf of Aden to "ensure the safety of commercial ships of our country against the threat of pirates", according to a rear admiral.[^cite_note-271] According to the same source, the deployment was scheduled to last until mid-June. Iran's deputy foreign minister, [Hossein Amir-Abdollahian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hossein_Amir-Abdollahian), told state-run [Tasnim News Agency](42800472.md), "others will not be allowed to put our shared security at risk with military adventures".[^cite_note-272]

To address continued disruption of commercial shipping under the coalition blockade, the United Nations Office for Project Services (UNOPS) operationalized the UN Verification and Inspection Mechanism for Yemen (UNVIM) on 2 May 2016. Based in Djibouti, UNVIM was tasked with clearing cargo vessels bound for Houthi-held Red Sea ports under UN Security Council Resolution 2216.[^cite_note-UN-press-2016-273] By the end of 2016 the mechanism had cleared several hundred vessels carrying millions of tonnes of food, fuel and other supplies, though [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) documented that the coalition continued to delay or divert fuel tankers even after UNVIM had granted clearance.[^cite_note-HRW-2017-274] Restrictions tightened sharply in November 2017, when the coalition halted nearly all shipping to Houthi-controlled ports for roughly three weeks following a ballistic missile fired toward Riyadh, deepening fuel and food shortages before a partial reopening later that month.[^cite_note-HRW-2017-274]

A planned coalition offensive against the port city of Hodeidah was averted when government and Houthi delegations reached the Stockholm Agreement on 13 December 2018, providing for a ceasefire and mutual redeployment of forces from Hodeidah and the smaller ports of Salif and Ras Isa.[^cite_note-CrisisGroup-Stockholm-275] A UN mission (UNMHA) was deployed to monitor the agreement, and although full redeployment of forces was never completed, the ceasefire around the port largely held even as fighting continued on other fronts.[^cite_note-UN-Hodeidah-2019-276]

A more substantial easing followed the UN-brokered nationwide truce that took effect on 2 April 2022. Fuel tankers were thereafter permitted into Hodeidah largely without the delays in coalition "holding areas" that had previously been routine, and by early 2023 UNVIM data showed no fuel or bulk food vessel had been denied clearance to the port for almost a year.[^cite_note-RAND-truce-277] The truce formally lapsed on 2 October 2022 after the warring parties failed to agree on an extension, but neither side resumed cross-border drone or missile attacks, and the calm at sea persisted in practice into 2023.[^cite_note-UNNews-truceend-278]

#### Houthi attacks on shipping and the Red Sea crisis (2023–2025)

| icon | This section **may incorporate text from a [large language model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Large_language_models), which is [prohibited in Wikipedia articles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:LLM)**. Main article: [Red Sea crisis](75207766.md)

The nature of naval involvement in the Yemen conflict was transformed by the Gaza war, which began in October 2023. As the Houthis began launching missiles and drones at Israel, several were intercepted by US and allied naval and air defences operating in the Red Sea.[^cite_note-CFR-tracker-279] On 19 November 2023, Houthi fighters used a helicopter to board and seize the car carrier *Galaxy Leader* in the southern Red Sea, detaining its 25 crew members; the Houthis cited the vessel's links to an Israeli businessman, while Western officials suggested an Iranian intelligence-gathering ship in the area may have helped identify the target.[^cite_note-National-GalaxyLeader-280] The seizure opened a sustained campaign against shipping the group deemed Israeli-linked, which within weeks expanded to vessels connected to dozens of other nationalities.[^cite_note-CFR-tracker-279]

The United States responded by announcing the multinational Operation Prosperity Guardian on 18 December 2023 to escort merchant shipping, eventually drawing participation from more than twenty countries.[^cite_note-Wilson-timeline-281] On 11–12 January 2024 the United States and United Kingdom, with backing from several allies, launched Operation Poseidon Archer, striking some 60 Houthi missile, radar and storage sites with more than 150 precision-guided munitions; further rounds of strikes followed through 2024 under the same operation.[^cite_note-Wilson-timeline-281] Days later the UN Security Council adopted a resolution condemning the attacks and affirming the right of states to defend commercial and naval vessels transiting the Red Sea.[^cite_note-SCR-2026-282] On 19 February 2024 the European Union launched its own, explicitly defensive naval mission, EUNAVFOR Aspides, headquartered in Larissa, Greece, to escort merchant vessels and build maritime situational awareness without striking targets ashore.[^cite_note-EEAS-Aspides-283]

Despite this naval build-up, Houthi attacks continued and at times escalated. The bulk carrier *Rubymar* sank off the Yemeni coast in March 2024 after being struck, the first vessel lost in the campaign.[^cite_note-CrisisGroup-RedSea-284] The bulk carrier *Tutor* sank in June 2024 after an attack involving an uncrewed surface vessel and missiles that killed a crew member, then the most sophisticated strike of the campaign.[^cite_note-WashInstitute-Tutor-285] By October 2024 the United States estimated the Houthis had launched nearly 190 attacks on commercial and naval vessels since the campaign began, and container shipping capacity transiting the Red Sea was running roughly 64 percent below pre-crisis levels.[^cite_note-HoC-library-286]

A Gaza ceasefire in January 2025 briefly stilled the Houthi campaign,[^cite_note-CrisisGroup-RedSea-284] and on 22 January the group released the *Galaxy Leader*'s crew after 14 months in captivity, in a handover mediated by Oman.[^cite_note-PBS-GalaxyRelease-287] When that ceasefire later broke down, the United States launched an expanded campaign of strikes, Operation Rough Rider, from March to May 2025 in an effort to compel a lasting end to the attacks.[^cite_note-CFR-AnotherHormuz-288] The campaign ended in May 2025 with an Oman-brokered agreement under which the Houthis agreed to stop targeting US vessels in exchange for a halt to US strikes, though the deal did not extend to Israeli-linked shipping.[^cite_note-CFR-AnotherHormuz-288] Attacks on other shipping resumed in July 2025: early that month the Houthis sank two further bulk carriers, the *Magic Seas* and the *Eternity C*, killing four of the latter's crew and taking eleven others captive, before declaring a "fourth phase" of their campaign targeting any vessel whose operating company had dealings with Israeli ports, regardless of flag.[^cite_note-PBS-fourthphase-289]

The campaign wound down again after a further Gaza ceasefire took hold in October 2025; the Houthis suspended attacks on shipping and Israel,[^cite_note-CRS-USNI-290] and in November formally announced an end to the campaign and the lifting of their blockade on Israeli ports.[^cite_note-MaritimeExec-291]

#### Renewed tensions amid the 2026 Iran war

The reason given is: **see [WP\:AINB § User\:Yahya0071](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:AINB#User\:Yahya0071)** *(August 2026)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

By January 2026, the lull had allowed major carriers including Maersk and CMA CGM to resume scheduled Red Sea services, but the calm proved temporary.[^cite_note-FreightWaves-292] After Israel and the United States launched strikes on Iran in late February 2026, opening the 2026 Iran war, the Houthis warned on 28 February that they would resume attacks on Israel and on Red Sea shipping.[^cite_note-FreightWaves-292] The group resumed direct strikes on Israel on 28 March 2026, and separately warned it might close the Bab-el-Mandeb strait if Gulf Arab states joined the war against Iran, raising fears of a second maritime chokepoint crisis alongside Iran's disruption of the Strait of Hormuz.[^cite_note-JPost-missiles-293][^cite_note-FoxNews-chokepoint-294] In practice, the group limited itself to a small number of missile and drone strikes on Israel rather than a renewed large-scale maritime campaign, restraint that analysts linked to pressure from regional actors and concern over the group's domestic standing in Yemen.[^cite_note-FoxNews-chokepoint-294]

Following a ceasefire between the United States, Israel and Iran, the Houthis kept up only sporadic strikes on Israel while reportedly using the lull to improve the range and accuracy of their missile arsenal, according to Western intelligence sources.[^cite_note-JPost-missiles-293] On 8 June 2026 the group announced a renewed, complete ban on Israeli-flagged or Israeli-linked vessels transiting the Red Sea, branding them "legitimate military targets", even as its wider campaign against non-Israeli shipping remained suspended.[^cite_note-CFR-AnotherHormuz-288] European and other coalition naval forces remained deployed in the area under extended mandates; the EU's Operation Aspides mission, for instance, was extended in February 2026 through February 2027.[^cite_note-Consilium-2026-295] As of late June 2026, with the Strait of Hormuz only slowly reopening under its own ceasefire, the prospect of renewed large-scale Houthi attacks on the Red Sea continued to weigh on global shipping and energy markets.[^cite_note-CFR-AnotherHormuz-288]

### Scale and participation of Saudi-led coalition members

Pakistan was called on by Saudi Arabia to join the coalition, but its parliament voted to maintain neutrality.[^cite_note-296] In February 2016, [Constellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constellis) (commonly known as Blackwater, named Academi at the time) withdrew from front-line duties in the Yemen campaign.[^cite_note-arwa1-78] Qatar was suspended from the coalition due to the [2017 Qatar diplomatic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Qatar_diplomatic_crisis).[^cite_note-297] [Morocco](19291.md) ended their participation in 2019 due to deterioration of [Morocco–Saudi Arabia relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morocco%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_relations)[^cite_note-298] followed by [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) in July 2019 amid possible tensions with Iran on the Persian Gulf and differences with Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-auto1-4] Sudan announced its decision to reduce troops commitment from 15,000 to 5,000 in early December 2019.[^cite_note-299]

Saudi Arabia had 2,500 troops deployed for Operation Restoring Hope as of 2024, a total of two armored brigades from the [Royal Saudi Land Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Saudi_Land_Forces), according to the [International Institute for Strategic Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Strategic_Studies).[^cite_note-IISS2024-44]

The Saudi-led campaign has received [widespread criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_Saudi_Arabian-led_intervention_in_Yemen_\(2015%E2%80%93present\)) and had a dramatic worsening effect on the humanitarian situation in Yemen, that reached the level of a "humanitarian disaster"[^cite_note-auto9-97] or "humanitarian catastrophe". The war has contributed to the [famine in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Famine_in_Yemen) which has threatened over 17 million people, according to the UN, as well as an [outbreak of cholera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016%E2%80%9317_Yemen_cholera_outbreak) which has infected hundreds of thousands.[^cite_note-un-org_2015-07-28_DCN-96][^cite_note-ECHAO_2015-12-14_ECS-301][^cite_note-BBCNews_2015-08-19_YCH-302]

After the Saudi-led coalition declared the entire [Saada Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saada_Governorate) a military target in May 2015, the UN's Humanitarian Coordinator for Yemen and Human Rights Watch expressed concern that the bombing there was unnecessarily harming civilians.[^cite_note-ReutersUS_2015-05-09_SLS-303][^cite_note-OCHAYemen_2015-05-09_SBH-304] On 1 July 2015, the UN declared for Yemen a "level-three" emergency—the highest UN emergency level—for a period of six months.[^cite_note-UNOCHA_2015-11-22_2HNO-305][^cite_note-unocha-org_2015-07-01_HER-306] Human rights groups repeatedly blamed the Saudi-led military coalition for killing civilians and destroying health centres and other infrastructure with airstrikes.[^cite_note-NYT_2015-12-22_MAY-307]

&#x20;In June 2015, aid agencies said the de facto [blockade](293054.md) of Yemen had dramatically worsened the humanitarian situation, with 78% (20 million) of the population in urgent need of food, water and medical aid. Aid ships are allowed, but the bulk of commercial shipping, on which the country relies, is blocked.[^cite_note-Borger-308] In one incident, coalition jets prevented an [Iranian Red Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Red_Crescent) plane from landing by bombing [Sanaa International Airport](4202141.md)'s runway, which blocked aid delivery by air.[^cite_note-The_Guardian-309]



Protest against the military intervention in Yemen on 14 August 2020

According to [Farea Al-Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farea_Al-Muslim), direct war crimes were committed during the conflict; for example, an [IDP (internally displaced person)](427408.md) camp was hit by a Saudi airstrike, while Houthis sometimes prevented aid workers from giving aid.[^cite_note-310] The UN and human rights groups discussed the possibility that war crimes may have been committed by Saudi Arabia during the air campaign.[^cite_note-311]

US Representative [Ted Lieu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Lieu) has criticized the Saudi-led attacks on Yemen: "Some of these strikes look like war crimes to me, and I want to get answers as to why the US appears to be assisting in the execution of war crimes in Yemen."[^cite_note-lieu-312]

In March 2017, [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) (HRW) reported, "Since the start of the current conflict, at least 4,773 civilians had been killed and 8,272 wounded, the majority by coalition airstrikes.... Human Rights Watch has documented 62 apparently unlawful coalition airstrikes, some of which may amount to war crimes, that have killed nearly 900 civilians, and documented seven indiscriminate attacks by Houthi-Saleh forces in Aden and Taizz that killed 139 people, including at least eight children."[^cite_note-313]

In an April 2020 report, Human Rights Watch said that war crimes committed by Saudi Arabia and United Arab Emirates in Yemen go unmentioned. They stated that these countries were responsible for most child casualties and illegal attacks on schools.[^cite_note-314] On December third, 2020, more than 60 organizations urged the U.N. General Assembly to establish an investigative body to gather and preserve evidence of serious human rights violations during Yemen's seven-year conflict, including possible war crimes and crimes against humanity.[^cite_note-315]

On 20 December 2021, the Saudi-led coalition carried out air raids at the international airport in the Yemeni capital, [Sanaa](18691665.md). As a result of these airstrikes, [UN](31769.md) aid flights into Sanaa were halted as the airport was no longer able to receive aircraft operated by the United Nations or international humanitarian organisations. Since 2016, humanitarian flights into Sanaa airport have been largely interrupted by a Saudi-led blockade.[^cite_note-316]

According to a report released in December 2022 by the Largest Dutch and Yemeni humanitarian organizations, [PAX](https://paxforpeace.nl/) and [Mwatana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radhya_Al-Mutawakel#Founding_and_work_with_Mwatana_Organisation_for_Human_Rights), the Houthis and the Saudi/UAE-led coalition have used explosive weapons in densely populated areas "extensively" throughout the duration of the Yemen conflict.[^cite_note-317] In the report it is said that Mwatana had independently documented 1,044 coalition airstrikes that caused 7,591 [civilian casualties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civilian_casualty), and 805 ground-launched attacks perpetrated by both parties that resulted in 2,636 civilian casualties by August 2022. The further accusation is made that the Saudi/UAE-led coalition's use of guided missiles against civilian targets is in breach of [International humanitarian law](1093604.md), a claim evidenced through photos containing the remnants of 2 [GBU-12 Paveway IIs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GBU-12_Paveway_II) supposedly in 2 family houses where 15 innocents are said to have died.[^cite_note-318] The 3 instances discussed in that report are in addition to the 27 Mwatana had already provided in a report published 2 year prior with the [University Network for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cavallaro) showing [Precision-guided munition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precision-guided_munition) remnants present in alleged [war crimes](48626.md) targeting 16 "civilian gatherings and structures," 5 "educational and health facilities," 5 "civilian businesses" and a "government cultural center" where "at least 203" civilians died is claimed to suggest "the Saudi/UAE-led Coalition airstrikes ... violate [IHL](1093604.md)'s requirements of [distinction](1093604.md#Principle_of_distinction) and [proportionality](1093604.md#Necessity_and_proportionality)."[^cite_note-319]

On August 21, 2023, *The Washington Post*[^cite_note-320] reported that Saudi security forces killed hundreds of Ethiopian migrants and asylum seekers attempting to cross the country's border with Yemen, according to Human Rights Watch. The Saudis allegedly shot at people at close range and fired explosive weapons at groups in the mountains, in what could amount to crimes against humanity.

### Attacks on facilities run by aid organizations

Since the Saudi-led coalition began military operations against Houthi on 26 March 2015, Saudi-led coalition airstrikes unlawfully struck hospitals and other facilities run by aid organizations, according to Human Rights Watch.[^cite_note-HRW_2016-02-17_SWN-321] [Médecins Sans Frontières](20498.md) (MSF) medical facilities in Yemen were attacked four times in three months.[^cite_note-MSF_2016-01-25_HFU-322] On 26 October 2015, HRW documented six Saudi-led airstrikes which bombed a MSF hospital in Haydan district (Saada Governorate), wounding two patients.[^cite_note-HRW_2016-02-17_SWN-321][^cite_note-MSF_2016-01-25_HFU-322][^cite_note-323] MSF's director of operations Raquel Ayora said: "The way war is being waged in Yemen is causing enormous suffering and shows that the warring parties do not recognise or respect the protected status of hospitals and medical facilities. We witness the devastating consequences of this on people trapped in conflict zones on a daily basis. Nothing has been spared—not even hospitals, even though medical facilities are explicitly protected by international humanitarian law."[^cite_note-MSF_2016-01-25_HFU-322]

### Saudi Arabia's response to accusations

On 16 May 2016, [Brigadier General Ahmed Hassan Asiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Asiri_\(military\)) responded to Human Rights Watch's accusations, stating that Saudi Arabia's actions are not motivated by self-interest, but rather "because we saw population undermined and oppressed by the militias".[^cite_note-npr.org-324] Ahmed Asiri claimed that [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) did not have a team on the ground in Yemen, and when told by [Mary Louise Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Louise_Kelly) during an interview that Human Rights Watch had visited Yemen, stated "No. No one can get in Yemen without the permission of the coalition".[^cite_note-npr.org-324]

Human Rights Watch responded to these statements on 16 May 2016. Belkis Wille stated, "In fact, this two-week trip was the fourth I had made to Yemen since the beginning of the war in March 2015. Given what I go through to get into Yemen, al-Assiri's statement was laughable".[^cite_note-hrw\.org2-325] She stated that on each of her visits to Yemen during this time period her passport has been confiscated, with no reason being given. She claims that this indicates that the coalition knows that she is visiting Yemen.[^cite_note-hrw\.org2-325]

After initially denying responsibility, on 15 October 2016, Saudi Arabia admitted responsibility for the funeral airstrikes which killed at least 140 and injured 525.[^cite_note-guardian-326] Saudi Arabian forces blamed the airstrikes on "wrong information" which was provided by an unnamed party, which had reportedly claimed the funeral was a legitimate target.[^cite_note-guardian-326] Human Rights Watch has claimed that the airstrikes likely constitute a war crime, due to the indiscriminate nature of the attack.[^cite_note-327]
